AP Calculus AB - Unit 1 Canonical Content Bank

Limits and Continuity | Course Profile: MATH_AP_CALCULUS

What's To Come

Section 1.1: What Is a Limit?

U1-S1-WTC01
Section 1.1 | What's To Come | n-a | DOK 2

A moving object has position $s(t)=t^2-2t+4$. The table shows secant slopes from $t=3$ to nearby times.

Nearby time tSecant slope
2.53.5
2.93.9
2.993.99
3.014.01
3.14.1
3.54.5
  1. What number do the secant slopes appear to approach as the nearby time approaches $3$?
  2. Write a limit statement that captures the observed approach.
  3. Explain in one sentence how this connects average rate of change to an instantaneous rate of change.
Answer / Solution:
  1. The slopes approach $4$.
  2. $\lim_{t\to3}\frac{s(t)-s(3)}{t-3}=4$.
  3. Average rates over shrinking intervals approach the instantaneous rate at $t=3$.

Section 1.2: Limits from Graphs and Tables

U1-S2-WTC01
Section 1.2 | What's To Come | n-a | DOK 2

The graph shows a function near $x=2$.

  1. Estimate $\lim_{x\to2^-}f(x)$ and $\lim_{x\to2^+}f(x)$.
  2. Decide whether $\lim_{x\to2}f(x)$ exists.
  3. Explain why the plotted value $f(2)$ does or does not settle the limit question.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $4$ and $1$.
  2. The two-sided limit does not exist because the one-sided limits differ.
  3. The function value is separate evidence; the limit is determined by nearby behavior.

Section 1.3: Algebraic Limits

U1-S3-WTC01
Section 1.3 | What's To Come | n-a | DOK 3

Without evaluating them completely at first, sort the following limits by the first method you would try: direct substitution, factoring, conjugate, or trigonometric/squeeze reasoning.

  1. $\lim_{x\to2}(x^3-4x+1)$
  2. $\lim_{x\to3}\frac{x^2-9}{x-3}$
  3. $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{4+x}-2}{x}$
  4. $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin(5x)}{x}$

Then explain what feature of each expression led to your choice.

Answer / Solution:
  1. Direct substitution; the expression is continuous at 2.
  2. Factoring; substitution gives $\frac00$ and the numerator factors.
  3. Conjugate; rationalization removes the $\frac00$ form.
  4. Trigonometric limit structure; rewrite to use $\lim_{u\to0}\frac{\sin u}{u}=1$.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-WTC01
Section 1.4 | What's To Come | n-a | DOK 3

Consider $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+3,&x\lt 2\k,&x=2\x^2-1,&x\gt 2\end{cases}$.

  1. Find the left-hand and right-hand limits at $x=2$.
  2. Is there any value of $k$ that makes the function continuous at $2$? Justify.
  3. If continuity is impossible, name the condition that fails first.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $5$ from the left and $3$ from the right.
  2. No. Changing $f(2)$ cannot make unequal one-sided limits agree.
  3. The two-sided limit does not exist because the one-sided limits differ.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-WTC01
Section 1.5 | What's To Come | n-a | DOK 3

A continuous function $g$ on $[1,6]$ satisfies $g(1)=-3$ and $g(6)=5$. A second function $h(x)=2+\frac{4}{x-3}$ is defined for $x e3$.

  1. What value is guaranteed to occur as an output of $g$ between $x=1$ and $x=6$? State a theorem that supports your claim.
  2. Describe the vertical and horizontal asymptotic behavior of $h$ using limit notation.
  3. Identify which conclusion depends on continuity and which depends on end or infinite-limit behavior.
Answer / Solution:
  1. For example $0$ is guaranteed because it lies between $-3$ and $5$; IVT applies on $[1,6]$.
  2. $\lim_{x\to3^-}h(x)=-\infty$, $\lim_{x\to3^+}h(x)=\infty$, and $\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}h(x)=2$.
  3. The IVT conclusion uses continuity; the asymptote conclusions use infinite and end-behavior limits.

Notes Examples

Section 1.1: What Is a Limit?

U1-S1-NOTE-EX01
Section 1.1 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 1

Interpret each piece of the statement $\lim_{x\to4}f(x)=9$.

  1. What does $x\qquad o4$ describe?
  2. What does the value $9$ describe?
  3. Does the statement force $f(4)=9$? Explain.
Answer / Solution:
  1. Inputs approach 4.
  2. Nearby outputs approach 9.
  3. No. A limit can be 9 even if $f(4)$ is different or undefined.
U1-S1-NOTE-EX02
Section 1.1 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

Estimate the limit represented by the table.

xf(x)
2.807.910
2.957.978
2.997.995
3.018.005
3.058.022
3.208.090
  1. State the estimate.
  2. Write it in limit notation.
  3. Name one reason the table supports an estimate rather than an exact proof.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $8$.
  2. $\lim_{x\to3}f(x)=8$.
  3. The table samples only selected nearby inputs rather than all inputs arbitrarily close to 3.
U1-S1-NOTE-EX03
Section 1.1 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

For $s(t)=t^2+1$ compare average and instantaneous change near $t=2$.

  1. Find the average rate of change from $t=2$ to $t=2.5$.
  2. Find it from $t=2$ to $t=2.1$.
  3. What value do these slopes suggest as the interval shrinks toward $t=2$?
Answer / Solution:
  1. $4.5$.
  2. $4.1$.
  3. The slopes approach $4$.
U1-S1-NOTE-EX04
Section 1.1 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

Use the graph to distinguish a function value from a limit.

  1. Estimate $\lim_{x\to1}f(x)$.
  2. Read $f(1)$ from the graph.
  3. Explain why the two answers can differ.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $3$.
  2. $6$.
  3. A limit describes nearby outputs; the function value is the output at the point itself.

For $p(t)=t^2-3t$ compute the average rate of change from $t=4$ to $4.4$ and from $4$ to $4.05$. What instantaneous rate is suggested?

  2. Find the right-hand limit.
  3. Determine the two-sided limit.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $4$.
  2. $1$.
  3. DNE because the one-sided limits disagree.

xf(x)

1.995.995
2.016.005

  1. Estimate each one-sided limit.
  2. State the two-sided limit.
  3. Does the table determine $f(2)$?

  1. $6$ from each side.
  2. $6$.

U1-S2-NOTE-EX03

xf(x)
0.801.950
0.951.988
0.991.998
1.014.998
1.054.990
1.204.960
  1. What do the left-side values approach?
  2. What do the right-side values approach?

  2. $5$.
  3. A two-sided limit exists only when both one-sided limits agree.
U1-S2-NOTE-EX04
Section 1.2 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 3

Two representations are intended to describe the same function near $x=3$. A graph indicates a left-hand limit of $4$ and a right-hand limit of $4$. A calculator table shows nearby values $3.98,3.996,4.004,4.02$.

  1. State the limit supported by both representations.
  2. Explain what agreement between the representations contributes to your confidence.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $\lim_{x\to3}f(x)=4$.
  2. Both graphical and numerical evidence point to the same approached output from both sides.

U1-S2-NOTE-YTI03

Notes Examples

Section 1.3: Algebraic Limits

U1-S3-NOTE-EX01
Section 1.3 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 1

Evaluate using direct substitution or limit laws as appropriate.

  1. $\lim_{x\to2}(3x^2-x+1)$
  2. $\lim_{x\to1}\frac{x^2+4}{x+2}$

  3. If $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=3$ and $\lim_{x\to c}g(x)=-2$ find $\lim_{x\to c}[2f(x)-g(x)]$.

Answer / Solution:
  1. $11$.
  2. $\frac53$.
  3. $8$.
U1-S3-NOTE-EX02
Section 1.3 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

Evaluate by algebraic cancellation, stating why cancellation is valid for nearby $x$ values.

  1. $\lim_{x\to4}\frac{x^2-16}{x-4}$
  2. $\lim_{x\to2}\frac{x^2-5x+6}{x-2}$
Answer / Solution:
  1. $8$ after factoring $(x-4)(x+4)$.
  2. $-1$ after factoring $(x-2)(x-3)$.
U1-S3-NOTE-EX03
Section 1.3 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

Use a conjugate or a standard trigonometric limit.

  1. $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{9+x}-3}{x}$
  2. $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin(4x)}{x}$
Answer / Solution:
  1. $\frac16$ after multiplying by the conjugate.
  2. $4$ by rewriting as $4\frac{\sin(4x)}{4x}$.
U1-S3-NOTE-EX04
Section 1.3 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 3

For each limit choose the most efficient valid approach and justify the choice.

  1. $\lim_{x\to0}x^2\cos(1/x)$
  2. $\lim_{x\to3}\frac{x^2-9}{\sqrt{x+6}-3}$
Answer / Solution:
  1. Squeeze Theorem: $-x^2\le x^2\cos(1/x)\le x^2$ and both bounds approach 0, so the limit is 0.
  2. Rationalize the denominator; the expression simplifies to $(x+3)(\sqrt{x+6}+3)$ for nearby $x e3$, so the limit is $36$.

Notes Examples

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-NOTE-EX01
Section 1.4 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

At $x=2$ suppose $f(2)=5$, $\lim_{x\to2^-}f(x)=5$, and $\lim_{x\to2^+}f(x)=5$. For a second function $g$, the one-sided limits are both 4 but $g(2)=7$.

  1. Which function is continuous at 2?
  2. Classify the issue for $g$.
  3. State the three continuity conditions.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $f$ is continuous at 2.
  2. $g$ has a removable discontinuity at 2.
  3. The function value exists; the two-sided limit exists; and the limit equals the function value.
U1-S4-NOTE-EX02
Section 1.4 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

Find $k$ so the piecewise function is continuous at $x=1$: $f(x)=\begin{cases}2x+k,&x\lt 1\x^2+4,&x\ge1\end{cases}$.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires $2+k=5$, so $k=3$.
U1-S4-NOTE-EX03
Section 1.4 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

Use the graph to classify the discontinuity at $x=0$ and cite the evidence that supports the classification.

Answer / Solution:Jump discontinuity: the one-sided limits are finite but unequal ($2$ and $5$).
U1-S4-NOTE-EX04
Section 1.4 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

For $p(x)=\frac{x^2-9}{x-3}$ defined only by the displayed formula, determine where it is continuous and state how to remove any discontinuity.

Answer / Solution:The simplified expression is $x+3$ for $x e3$. It is continuous on $(-\infty,3)\cup(3,\infty)$. Defining $p(3)=6$ removes the discontinuity.

Notes You Try Its

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-NOTE-YTI01
Section 1.4 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 2

At $x=-1$ a function has $h(-1)=2$ and both one-sided limits equal $6$. Is it continuous? Classify and justify.

Answer / Solution:No. The limit exists but is not equal to the function value, so the discontinuity is removable.
U1-S4-NOTE-YTI02
Section 1.4 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 2

Determine $a$ so $g(x)=\begin{cases}ax-1,&x\lt 2\x+5,&x\ge2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=2$.

Answer / Solution:$2a-1=7$ so $a=4$.
U1-S4-NOTE-YTI03
Section 1.4 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 2

Classify the discontinuity at $x=3$ and justify from the graph.

Answer / Solution:Removable discontinuity: both one-sided limits equal $-1$ but $f(3)=4$.
U1-S4-NOTE-YTI04
Section 1.4 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 2

For $q(x)=\frac{x^2-4}{x-2}$ state the intervals of continuity and the value that would make the function continuous at $x=2$.

Answer / Solution:Continuous on $(-\infty,2)\cup(2,\infty)$; define $q(2)=4$ to remove the hole.

Notes Examples

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-NOTE-EX01
Section 1.5 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

For $f(x)=\frac{3}{x-2}$ describe behavior at $x=2$ using one-sided infinite limits and identify the vertical asymptote.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to2^-}f(x)=-\infty$, $\lim_{x\to2^+}f(x)=\infty$; vertical asymptote $x=2$.
U1-S5-NOTE-EX02
Section 1.5 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

Determine the end behavior and horizontal asymptote of $h(x)=\frac{5x^2-2}{x^2+4}$.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to\infty}h(x)=5$ and $\lim_{x\to-\infty}h(x)=5$; horizontal asymptote $y=5$.
U1-S5-NOTE-EX03
Section 1.5 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 2

A function $f$ is continuous on $[1,4]$ with $f(1)=-2$ and $f(4)=6$.

  1. Does IVT guarantee a solution to $f(c)=3$ for some $c$ in $(1,4)$?
  2. Does it guarantee $f(c)=8$? Explain each answer.
Answer / Solution:
  1. Yes. 3 lies between -2 and 6 and continuity on the closed interval is given.
  2. No. 8 is not between the endpoint values, so IVT gives no such guarantee.
U1-S5-NOTE-EX04
Section 1.5 | Notes Examples | current-section | DOK 3

Critique the claim: "A horizontal asymptote $y=2$ means the graph can never cross $y=2$." Then decide whether IVT may be used on an interval that contains a vertical asymptote.

Answer / Solution:The claim is false: a horizontal asymptote describes end behavior and may be crossed. IVT cannot be invoked across a vertical asymptote because continuity on the interval fails.

Notes You Try Its

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-NOTE-YTI01
Section 1.5 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 2

For $g(x)=\frac{-4}{x+1}$ give the one-sided infinite limits at $x=-1$ and the vertical asymptote.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to-1^-}g(x)=\infty$, $\lim_{x\to-1^+}g(x)=-\infty$; vertical asymptote $x=-1$.
U1-S5-NOTE-YTI02
Section 1.5 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 2

Find both end-behavior limits and the horizontal asymptote of $p(x)=\frac{-2x^3+x}{x^3+7}$.

Answer / Solution:Both limits equal $-2$; horizontal asymptote $y=-2$.
U1-S5-NOTE-YTI03
Section 1.5 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 2

A continuous function on $[-2,5]$ has endpoint values $g(-2)=7$ and $g(5)=-1$. Determine whether IVT guarantees an input where $g(x)=4$ and whether it guarantees an input where $g(x)=9$.

Answer / Solution:It guarantees the value 4 because 4 lies between -1 and 7. It does not guarantee 9.
U1-S5-NOTE-YTI04
Section 1.5 | Notes You Try Its | current-section | DOK 3

Explain why $f(x)=2+\frac{\sin x}{x}$ can cross its horizontal asymptote $y=2$ while still satisfying $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=2$. Also state why a discontinuity inside $[a,b]$ blocks a direct IVT conclusion on that entire interval.

Answer / Solution:Crossing finitely or repeatedly does not contradict approaching 2 for large x. IVT requires continuity on the full closed interval.

U1-S1-PS1-Q11
Section 1.1 | Practice Set 1 | current-section | DOK 1

In $\lim_{x\to -1}f(x)=8$ identify the value approached by $x$ and the value approached by $f(x)$.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-PS1-Q01
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 1 | current-section | DOK 2

At $x=2$ suppose both one-sided limits equal $4$ and $f(2)=4$.

Is the function continuous at the point? State the conditions you checked.

Reasoning lens: Organize the conclusion using the three continuity conditions: defined value, existing limit, and equality.

Answer / Solution:Continuous. The two-sided limit is $4$ and the point value is $4$. Continuity requires equality between them.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-PS1-Q07

Identify the missing condition.

Reasoning lens: Organize the conclusion using the three continuity conditions: defined value, existing limit, and equality.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires the limit to exist and equal the function value. Here $4$ is not $6$, so the function is not continuous; the mismatch is removable if the limit is finite.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-PS1-Q01
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 1 | current-section | DOK 2

For $f(x)=\frac{2}{x-2}$ state the two one-sided infinite limits at $x=2$ and identify the vertical asymptote.

Reasoning lens: Express the asymptotic or IVT conclusion in limit/theorem language and cite the decisive condition.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to 2^-}f(x)=-\infty$, $\lim_{x\to 2^+}f(x)=\infty$, vertical asymptote $x=2$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS1-Q02

Reasoning lens: Express the asymptotic or IVT conclusion in limit/theorem language and cite the decisive condition.

Answer / Solution:Both end-behavior limits equal $3$; the horizontal asymptote is $y=3$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS1-Q04

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to 2^-}h(x)=-\infty$ and $\lim_{x\to 2^+}h(x)=\infty$ because the numerator is positive and the denominator changes sign.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS1-Q05
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 1 | current-section | DOK 2

A function satisfies $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=-1$. A student claims the graph can never cross $y=-1$.

Is the claim true or false?

Reasoning lens: Express the asymptotic or IVT conclusion in limit/theorem language and cite the decisive condition.

Answer / Solution:The claim is false. A horizontal asymptote describes limiting end behavior; crossings at finite inputs are allowed.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS1-Q06
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 1 | current-section | DOK 2

A function $F$ is continuous on $[0,5]$ with $F(0)=-2$ and $F(5)=5$. Does the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantee some $c$ in the interval with $F(c)=2$?

Reasoning lens: Express the asymptotic or IVT conclusion in limit/theorem language and cite the decisive condition.

Answer / Solution:Yes. Continuity on the closed interval is given, and $2$ lies between $-2$ and $5$. IVT guarantees at least one such input.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Reasoning lens: Express the asymptotic or IVT conclusion in limit/theorem language and cite the decisive condition.

Answer / Solution:No theorem guarantee can be made from IVT without continuity on the closed interval.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Reasoning lens: Add one sentence explaining what the notation or data does not allow you to conclude.

Answer / Solution:The average rate is $8.0$ because $\frac{38-6}{6-2}=8.0$.

U1-S3-PS2-Q03
Section 1.3 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 4}\frac{x^2-16}{x-4}$ by factoring.

Identify the removable factor before evaluating.

Reasoning lens: Identify the algebraic structure or theorem that makes your method valid.

Answer / Solution:Factor as $\frac{(x-4)(x+4)}{x-4}$ for nearby $x e 4$. The limit is $8$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 3}\frac{x^2-6x+9}{x-3}$.

Reasoning lens: Identify the algebraic structure or theorem that makes your method valid.

Answer / Solution:The numerator factors as $(x-3)(x-3)$. After cancellation the limit is $0$.

U1-S3-PS2-Q05

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{36+x}-6}{x}$.

Name the expression used to rationalize the numerator.

Reasoning lens: Identify the algebraic structure or theorem that makes your method valid.

Answer / Solution:Multiply by the conjugate. The expression becomes $\frac1{\sqrt{36+x}+6}$, so the limit is $\frac1{12}$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Answer / Solution:Both bounds approach 0, so the Squeeze Theorem gives $\lim_{x\to0}f(x)=0$.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-PS2-Q01
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

At $x=-1$ suppose both one-sided limits equal $5$ and $f(-1)=7$.

Identify the failed continuity condition.

Reasoning lens: Distinguish the function value from the limiting value before classifying the behavior.

Answer / Solution:Not continuous. The two-sided limit is $5$ and the point value is $7$. Continuity requires equality between them.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-PS2-Q02
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

Find $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}kx+1,&x\lt -1\\3x+2,&x\ge -1\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=-1$.

Reasoning lens: Distinguish the function value from the limiting value before classifying the behavior.

Answer / Solution:Set the two boundary expressions equal: $-1=k(-1)+1$. Thus $k=2$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-PS2-Q03

The formula $g(x)=\frac{x^2-16}{x-4}$ is used for $x\ne 4$. What value should be assigned to $g(4)$ to remove the discontinuity?

Reasoning lens: Distinguish the function value from the limiting value before classifying the behavior.

Answer / Solution:The nearby expression simplifies to $x+4$, so define $g(4)=8$.

U1-S4-PS2-Q05
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

State the intervals on which $h(x)=\frac{x+1}{x-4}$ is continuous.

Explain why the excluded point breaks continuity.

Reasoning lens: Distinguish the function value from the limiting value before classifying the behavior.

Answer / Solution:The rational function is continuous on its domain: $(-\infty,4)\cup(4,\infty)$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-PS2-Q06
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

At a piecewise boundary $x=-1$ the left branch approaches $5$ and the right branch approaches $6$.

Can any choice of the point value make the function continuous? Explain.

Reasoning lens: Distinguish the function value from the limiting value before classifying the behavior.

Answer / Solution:No point value can fix the jump because the one-sided limits differ.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-PS2-Q07
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

A student argues that a function is continuous at $x=-1$ because $f(-1)=7$ exists. The two-sided limit is $5$.

Rewrite the student argument as a correct continuity test.

Reasoning lens: Distinguish the function value from the limiting value before classifying the behavior.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires the limit to exist and equal the function value. Here $5$ is not $7$, so the function is not continuous; the mismatch is removable if the limit is finite.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-PS2-Q08

Use the graph to decide whether the function is continuous at $x=-1$.

Reasoning lens: Distinguish the function value from the limiting value before classifying the behavior.

Answer / Solution:Compare the left and right approaches first, then compare their common value (if any) with the filled point. Here the left approach is $5$ and the right approach is $5$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-PS2-Q01
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

For $f(x)=\frac{-2}{x--2}$ state the two one-sided infinite limits at $x=-2$ and identify the vertical asymptote.

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to -2^-}f(x)=\infty$, $\lim_{x\to -2^+}f(x)=-\infty$, vertical asymptote $x=-2$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS2-Q02
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

Determine $\lim_{x\to\infty}$ and $\lim_{x\to-\infty}$ for $g(x)=\frac{4x^2+1}{1x^2+4}$ and identify the horizontal asymptote.

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:Both end-behavior limits equal $4$; the horizontal asymptote is $y=4$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS2-Q03

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:The graph has vertical asymptote $x=-2$ and horizontal asymptote $y=3$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS2-Q04
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

For $h(x)=3+\frac{5}{x--2}$ determine the sign of the infinite limit from each side of $x=-2$.

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to -2^-}h(x)=-\infty$ and $\lim_{x\to -2^+}h(x)=\infty$ because the numerator is positive and the denominator changes sign.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS2-Q05
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

A function satisfies $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=3$. A student claims the graph can never cross $y=3$.

Give a conceptual counterargument.

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:The claim is false. A horizontal asymptote describes limiting end behavior; crossings at finite inputs are allowed.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS2-Q06
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 2

A function $F$ is continuous on $[0,6]$ with $F(0)=-2$ and $F(6)=5$. Does the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantee some $c$ in the interval with $F(c)=2$?

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:Yes. Continuity on the closed interval is given, and $2$ lies between $-2$ and $5$. IVT guarantees at least one such input.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS2-Q07
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 2 | current-section | DOK 3

$F$ is continuous on $[1,5]$ and $F(1)=-1$, but $F(5)$ is unknown. Can IVT guarantee an output of $2$?

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:Not from the given information; without the other endpoint value we cannot verify that $2$ lies between the endpoint outputs.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS2-Q09

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:For sufficiently large time/input, the modeled quantity can be made arbitrarily close to $3$ in the stated units; equality at a finite input is not required.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

A report claims IVT guarantees a solution to $F(x)=1$ somewhere in $(1,6)$. The only supplied facts are $F(1)=-3$ and $F(6)=6$.

List the two checks required before accepting the claim.

Reasoning lens: Separate what the graph or formula suggests from what the theorem actually guarantees.

Answer / Solution:Continuity on the closed interval $[1,6]$ is essential, and the target value must lie between the endpoint outputs. With both conditions verified, IVT guarantees at least one input.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Practice Set 3

Section 1.1: What Is a Limit?

U1-S1-PS3-Q01
Section 1.1 | Practice Set 3 | current-section | DOK 2

A student says $\lim_{x\to 4}g(x)=8$ means $g(4)=8$. Identify the error and rewrite the meaning correctly.

Reasoning lens: Compare the result with what a single point value alone would tell you.

Answer / Solution:A limit describes nearby behavior, not necessarily the value at the point. Inputs approach $4$ while outputs approach $8$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Near $x=2$ the values of $f(x)$ approach $5$ from both sides, but $f(2)=9$.

Explain why the function value does not change the limit.

Reasoning lens: Describe one additional nearby observation that would strengthen the conclusion.

Answer / Solution:The limit is $5$. The point value $9$ does not control nearby behavior.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Reasoning lens: Translate your conclusion into correct one-sided or two-sided notation as appropriate.

Answer / Solution:The two-sided limit is DNE because the one-sided limits differ. Changing the point value cannot repair that disagreement.

U1-S3-PS3-Q03
Section 1.3 | Practice Set 3 | current-section | DOK 2

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 5}\frac{x^2-25}{x-5}$ by factoring.

Explain why canceling the factor does not require the original expression to be defined at $x=5$.

Reasoning lens: State the transformed nearby expression or standard limit that justifies the final step.

Answer / Solution:Factor as $\frac{(x-5)(x+5)}{x-5}$ for nearby $x e 5$. The limit is $10$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Reasoning lens: State the transformed nearby expression or standard limit that justifies the final step.

Answer / Solution:The numerator factors as $(x-4)(x-3)$. After cancellation the limit is $1$.

U1-S3-PS3-Q05

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{49+x}-7}{x}$.

Show the equivalent expression after rationalization before taking the limit.

Reasoning lens: State the transformed nearby expression or standard limit that justifies the final step.

Answer / Solution:Multiply by the conjugate. The expression becomes $\frac1{\sqrt{49+x}+7}$, so the limit is $\frac1{14}$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S3-PS3-Q07
Section 1.3 | Practice Set 3 | current-section | DOK 2

For $x\ne0$ suppose $-|x|^2\le f(x)\le |x|^2$. Find $\lim_{x\to0}f(x)$ and name the theorem.

Explain why the two bounding limits are sufficient.

Reasoning lens: State the transformed nearby expression or standard limit that justifies the final step.

Answer / Solution:Both bounds approach 0, so the Squeeze Theorem gives $\lim_{x\to0}f(x)=0$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-PS3-Q01
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 3 | current-section | DOK 2

At $x=3$ suppose both one-sided limits equal $6$ and $f(3)=6$.

Write a compact three-condition continuity check.

Reasoning lens: Use interval or boundary language precisely and name the relevant domain restriction.

Answer / Solution:Continuous. The two-sided limit is $6$ and the point value is $6$. Continuity requires equality between them.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-PS3-Q02
Section 1.4 | Practice Set 3 | current-section | DOK 2

Find $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}kx+1,&x\lt 3\\4x+2,&x\ge 3\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=3$.

State the continuity equation before solving.

Reasoning lens: Use interval or boundary language precisely and name the relevant domain restriction.

Answer / Solution:Set the two boundary expressions equal: $14=k(3)+1$. Thus $k=4.33333$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Classify the discontinuity and justify.

Reasoning lens: Use interval or boundary language precisely and name the relevant domain restriction.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires the limit to exist and equal the function value. Here $6$ is not $8$, so the function is not continuous; the mismatch is removable if the limit is finite.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-PS3-Q01
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 3 | current-section | DOK 2

For $f(x)=\frac{2}{x-3}$ state the two one-sided infinite limits at $x=3$ and identify the vertical asymptote.

Reasoning lens: Support your answer with the relevant one-sided, end-behavior, or endpoint evidence.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to 3^-}f(x)=-\infty$, $\lim_{x\to 3^+}f(x)=\infty$, vertical asymptote $x=3$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Answer / Solution:Both end-behavior limits equal $5$; the horizontal asymptote is $y=5$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS3-Q05

A function satisfies $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=1$. A student claims the graph can never cross $y=1$.

Explain what a horizontal asymptote does and does not say.

Reasoning lens: Support your answer with the relevant one-sided, end-behavior, or endpoint evidence.

Answer / Solution:The claim is false. A horizontal asymptote describes limiting end behavior; crossings at finite inputs are allowed.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S5-PS3-Q06
Section 1.5 | Practice Set 3 | current-section | DOK 3

A function $F$ is continuous on $[0,7]$ with $F(0)=-2$ and $F(7)=5$. Does the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantee some $c$ in the interval with $F(c)=2$?

State each theorem condition you used.

Reasoning lens: Support your answer with the relevant one-sided, end-behavior, or endpoint evidence.

Answer / Solution:Yes. Continuity on the closed interval is given, and $2$ lies between $-2$ and $5$. IVT guarantees at least one such input.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Answer / Solution:No. 8 is not between the endpoint values -1 and 4, so IVT does not provide that guarantee.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Answer / Solution:For sufficiently large time/input, the modeled quantity can be made arbitrarily close to $1$ in the stated units; equality at a finite input is not required.

Answer / Solution:Continuity on the closed interval $[1,6]$ is essential, and the target value must lie between the endpoint outputs. With both conditions verified, IVT guarantees at least one input.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to 1^+}f(x)=7$. The superscript indicates the direction from which inputs approach.

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 6}\frac{x^2-36}{x-6}$ by factoring.

Compare factoring with direct substitution and explain why only one finishes the problem.

Reasoning lens: Check the result by naming the limit law, identity, or bound used in the last step.

Answer / Solution:Factor as $\frac{(x-6)(x+6)}{x-6}$ for nearby $x e 6$. The limit is $12$.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-XP-Q01
Section 1.4 | Extra Practice | current-section | DOK 3

At $x=1$ suppose both one-sided limits equal $7$ and $f(1)=9$.

Critique the claim "the two-sided limit exists, so continuity is automatic."

Reasoning lens: Identify a tempting incomplete continuity argument and explain what condition it omits.

Answer / Solution:Not continuous. The two-sided limit is $7$ and the point value is $9$. Continuity requires equality between them.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-XP-Q02
Section 1.4 | Extra Practice | current-section | DOK 2

Find $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}kx+1,&x\lt 1\\5x+2,&x\ge 1\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=1$.

State the continuity equation before solving.

Reasoning lens: Identify a tempting incomplete continuity argument and explain what condition it omits.

Answer / Solution:Set the two boundary expressions equal: $7=k(1)+1$. Thus $k=6$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

U1-S4-XP-Q07
Section 1.4 | Extra Practice | current-section | DOK 3

A student argues that a function is continuous at $x=1$ because $f(1)=9$ exists. The two-sided limit is $7$.

Explain why existence of a function value is necessary but not sufficient.

Reasoning lens: Identify a tempting incomplete continuity argument and explain what condition it omits.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires the limit to exist and equal the function value. Here $7$ is not $9$, so the function is not continuous; the mismatch is removable if the limit is finite.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Reasoning lens: Identify a tempting incomplete continuity argument and explain what condition it omits.

Answer / Solution:Compare the left and right approaches first, then compare their common value (if any) with the filled point. Here the left approach is $7$ and the right approach is $7$.

Reasoning check: A complete response should address the stated lens using the controlling definition, representation feature, algebraic structure, or theorem condition.

Show what you know:

A function satisfies $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=2$. A student claims the graph can never cross $y=2$.

Explain what a horizontal asymptote does and does not say.

Evidence request: Conclude with one sentence that links the local calculation to the larger limit or continuity idea.

Answer / Solution:The claim is false. A horizontal asymptote describes limiting end behavior; crossings at finite inputs are allowed.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

U1-S5-CYU-Q05
Section 1.5 | Check Your Understanding | unit-to-date | DOK 2

Show what you know:

Determine $\lim_{x\to\infty}$ and $\lim_{x\to-\infty}$ for $g(x)=\frac{6x^2+1}{1x^2+4}$ and identify the horizontal asymptote.

Evidence request: Conclude with one sentence that links the local calculation to the larger limit or continuity idea.

Answer / Solution:Both end-behavior limits equal $6$; the horizontal asymptote is $y=6$.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

U1-S5-CYU-Q06
Section 1.5 | Check Your Understanding | unit-to-date | DOK 2

Show what you know:

A model for drug concentration after repeated dosing has $\lim_{t\to\infty}M(t)=-1$. Interpret the limit in context without saying the quantity must ever equal $-1$.

Evidence request: Conclude with one sentence that links the local calculation to the larger limit or continuity idea.

Answer / Solution:For sufficiently large time/input, the modeled quantity can be made arbitrarily close to $-1$ in the stated units; equality at a finite input is not required.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Answer / Solution:The limit is $3$. The point value $6$ does not control nearby behavior.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Two students evaluate $\lim_{x\to 5}\frac{x^2-25}{x-5}$. Student A substitutes first and stops at $\frac00$. Student B factors and cancels a common factor for nearby inputs.

Complete Student B's work and critique Student A's stopping point.

Evidence request: State the mathematical conclusion first, then cite one decisive piece of evidence.

Answer / Solution:Student B is valid. $\frac00$ is indeterminate, not an answer. Factoring gives a nearby equivalent expression and the limit $10$.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-WU2-UTD01

Quick check:

Consider $p(x)=\frac{(x-5)(x+4)}{x-5}$ with its original domain.

Describe the graph of the simplified expression while preserving the original domain.

Evidence request: State the mathematical conclusion first, then cite one decisive piece of evidence.

Answer / Solution:The function equals $x+4$ for $x\ne 5$ and has a removable discontinuity at $x=5$. The missing limiting value is $9$.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-WU2-UTD01
Section 1.5 | Warm-Up 2 Academic Content | unit-to-date | DOK 3

Quick check:

A function $F$ is continuous on $[0,8]$ with $F(0)=-2$ and $F(8)=5$. Does the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantee some $c$ in the interval with $F(c)=2$?

State each theorem condition you used.

Evidence request: State the mathematical conclusion first, then cite one decisive piece of evidence.

Answer / Solution:Yes. Continuity on the closed interval is given, and $2$ lies between $-2$ and $5$. IVT guarantees at least one such input.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

U1-S5-WU2-UTD02
Section 1.5 | Warm-Up 2 Academic Content | unit-to-date | DOK 2

Quick check:

Use the graph to estimate the vertical and horizontal asymptotes.

Evidence request: State the mathematical conclusion first, then cite one decisive piece of evidence.

Answer / Solution:The graph has vertical asymptote $x=-2$ and horizontal asymptote $y=3$.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

U1-S5-WU2-UTD03
Section 1.5 | Warm-Up 2 Academic Content | unit-to-date | DOK 3

Quick check:

A report claims IVT guarantees a solution to $F(x)=2$ somewhere in $(1,6)$. The only supplied facts are $F(1)=-2$ and $F(6)=6$.

List the two checks required before accepting the claim.

Evidence request: State the mathematical conclusion first, then cite one decisive piece of evidence.

Answer / Solution:Continuity on the closed interval $[1,6]$ is essential, and the target value must lie between the endpoint outputs. With both conditions verified, IVT guarantees at least one input.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

U1-S5-WU2-UTD04
Section 1.5 | Warm-Up 2 Academic Content | unit-to-date | DOK 3

Unit-to-date retrieval from Section 1.4:

Quick check:

A student argues that a function is continuous at $x=3$ because $f(3)=8$ exists. The two-sided limit is $6$.

Classify the discontinuity and justify.

Evidence request: State the mathematical conclusion first, then cite one decisive piece of evidence.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires the limit to exist and equal the function value. Here $6$ is not $8$, so the function is not continuous; the mismatch is removable if the limit is finite.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-WU3-UTD01
Section 1.5 | Warm-Up 3 Academic Content | unit-to-date | DOK 3

Quick check:

A function $F$ is continuous on $[0,8]$ with $F(0)=-2$ and $F(8)=5$. Does the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantee some $c$ in the interval with $F(c)=2$?

State each theorem condition you used.

Evidence request: After solving, identify one tempting alternative conclusion and explain why it is not justified.

Answer / Solution:Yes. Continuity on the closed interval is given, and $2$ lies between $-2$ and $5$. IVT guarantees at least one such input.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Evidence request: After solving, identify one tempting alternative conclusion and explain why it is not justified.

Answer / Solution:The graph has vertical asymptote $x=-2$ and horizontal asymptote $y=3$.

U1-S5-WU3-UTD03

Quick check:

A report claims IVT guarantees a solution to $F(x)=2$ somewhere in $(1,6)$. The only supplied facts are $F(1)=-2$ and $F(6)=6$.

List the two checks required before accepting the claim.

Evidence request: After solving, identify one tempting alternative conclusion and explain why it is not justified.

Answer / Solution:Continuity on the closed interval $[1,6]$ is essential, and the target value must lie between the endpoint outputs. With both conditions verified, IVT guarantees at least one input.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Answer / Solution:Both bounds approach 0, so the Squeeze Theorem gives $\lim_{x\to0}f(x)=0$.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-ET-A-C01
Section 1.4 | Exit Ticket A | current-section | DOK 3

Exit evidence:

A student argues that a function is continuous at $x=3$ because $f(3)=8$ exists. The two-sided limit is $6$.

Classify the discontinuity and justify.

Evidence request: Name the single condition that would be most important to re-check if your answer were challenged.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires the limit to exist and equal the function value. Here $6$ is not $8$, so the function is not continuous; the mismatch is removable if the limit is finite.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

U1-S4-ET-A-C02
Section 1.4 | Exit Ticket A | current-section | DOK 2

Exit evidence:

The formula $g(x)=\frac{x^2-49}{x-7}$ is used for $x\ne 7$. What value should be assigned to $g(7)$ to remove the discontinuity?

Justify using a limit.

Evidence request: Name the single condition that would be most important to re-check if your answer were challenged.

Answer / Solution:The nearby expression simplifies to $x+7$, so define $g(7)=14$.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Evidence request: State the mathematical conclusion first, then cite one decisive piece of evidence.

Answer / Solution:The rational function is continuous on its domain: $(-\infty,3)\cup(3,\infty)$.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-ET-C-C01
Section 1.4 | Exit Ticket C | current-section | DOK 3

Exit evidence:

A student argues that a function is continuous at $x=3$ because $f(3)=8$ exists. The two-sided limit is $6$.

Classify the discontinuity and justify.

Evidence request: Use a second representation in words or notation to confirm the same mathematical conclusion.

Answer / Solution:Continuity requires the limit to exist and equal the function value. Here $6$ is not $8$, so the function is not continuous; the mismatch is removable if the limit is finite.

Evidence note: The response should explicitly satisfy the evidence request using the same controlling mathematics.

U1-S5-ET-A-C02
Section 1.5 | Exit Ticket A | current-section | DOK 3

Explain how the sign of the numerator and denominator controls your answer.

Evidence request: Name the single condition that would be most important to re-check if your answer were challenged.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to 2^-}h(x)=-\infty$ and $\lim_{x\to 2^+}h(x)=\infty$ because the numerator is positive and the denominator changes sign.

U1-S2-PERF01
Section 1.2 | Performance Tasks | performance | DOK 4

Limit Evidence Investigation

Investigate a hidden limiting value near $x=2$. Use the supplied sample values, create at least two additional input values on each side closer to 2, and organize your evidence.

xf(x)
1.85.64
1.955.91
2.056.10
2.26.44
  1. Estimate the limit and write it in notation.
  2. Sketch or describe the local graph suggested by your completed table.
  3. Identify any evidence that could challenge your first estimate and revise the claim if needed.
  4. State what additional evidence would make your conclusion stronger.
Answer / Solution:

Expected evidence: a defensible estimate of $6$ from both sides; student-generated inputs closer to 2; a graph or description consistent with approach toward 6; an explicit revision/check step.

Scoring criteria: 4 points - numerical evidence from both sides; 2 points - coherent representation; 2 points - limit notation and conclusion; 2 points - critique/revision statement.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-S4-PERF01
Section 1.4 | Performance Tasks | performance | DOK 4

Build a Continuity Model

Construct a piecewise function with three branches that satisfies all constraints:

  • continuous at $x=-1$;
  • a removable discontinuity at $x=2$ with limiting value $5$;
  • a jump discontinuity at $x=4$;
  • at least one nonconstant branch.
  1. Write the function with domains.
  2. Show limit and function-value checks at each specified boundary.
  3. Revise the model if any condition fails.
  4. Provide a simple sketch or value table that verifies the final model.
Answer / Solution:

Expected evidence: many models are possible. Each must satisfy all three local conditions and include verification.

Scoring criteria: 4 points - correct construction; 3 points - boundary checks; 2 points - representation check; 1 point - documented revision or validation.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-S5-PERF01
Section 1.5 | Performance Tasks | performance | DOK 4

Asymptotic Behavior Design Task

Design a rational function whose graph has vertical asymptote $x=3$ and horizontal asymptote $y=-2$. The graph must cross the horizontal asymptote at least once.

  1. Give a formula and its domain.
  2. Use limit notation to verify both asymptotes.
  3. Identify an input where the graph crosses $y=-2$.
  4. Modify one coefficient to move the vertical asymptote to $x=-1$ while keeping the same horizontal asymptote and explain the change.
Answer / Solution:

Expected evidence: one valid family is $f(x)=-2+\frac{x-a}{(x-3)(x-b)}$ with choices avoiding cancellation at $x=3$ and allowing a zero numerator at a finite input. Equivalent constructions are accepted.

Scoring criteria: 4 points - valid original model; 2 points - asymptote limits; 2 points - crossing verification; 2 points - successful revision.

U1-S5-PERF02
Section 1.5 | Performance Tasks | performance | DOK 4

Theorem Decision Performance

Four teams make IVT claims. Analyze each scenario and prepare a decision table with columns continuity verified?, target between endpoint outputs?, IVT guarantee?, and revision if unsupported.

  1. $f$ is continuous on $[0,4]$, $f(0)=-2$, $f(4)=7$, target $3$.
  2. $g(1)=-5$ and $g(6)=2$, target $0$, but continuity is not given.
  3. $h$ is continuous on $[-1,3]$, endpoints $4$ and $9$, target $2$.
  4. $p$ is continuous on $[2,8]$, endpoints $5$ and $-1$, target $0$.

After completing the table, write a short rule that another student could use to decide whether an IVT claim is justified.

Answer / Solution:

Expected decisions: (a) yes; (b) not guaranteed because continuity is missing; (c) not guaranteed because 2 is not between 4 and 9; (d) yes.

Scoring criteria: 2 points per scenario for condition analysis and conclusion; 2 points for a precise transferable decision rule.

U1-FA-D1-01-V1

Limit Notation and Reading Check - Version 1

Read $\lim_{x\to -2}f(x)=4$.

  1. Identify the approached input.
  2. Identify the approached output.
  3. State whether the notation alone determines $f(-2)$.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $-2$.
  2. $4$.
  3. No. The limit does not determine the point value.

Section 1.2 | Formative Assessment Source Content | performance | DOK 4

Limit Evidence Investigation - Version 1

Conduct a short limit-evidence investigation around $x=-2$.

xf(x)
-2.205.88
-2.055.97
-1.956.02
-1.806.10
  1. Generate two additional inputs on each side closer to $-2$.
  2. Predict corresponding values consistent with the observed trend or use a calculator model you construct.
  3. State and graph/describe a limit conjecture.
  4. Challenge the conjecture with one plausible conflicting datum and explain how you would decide whether to revise it.
Answer / Solution:

Method Selection for Algebraic Limits - Version 1

For each limit choose factoring, conjugate, or standard trigonometric-limit reasoning and carry out the method.

  1. $\lim_{x\to 3}\frac{x^2-9}{x-3}$
  2. $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{9+x}-3}{x}$
  3. $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin(3x)}{x}$
Answer / Solution:
  1. $6$.
  2. $\frac1{6}$.
  3. $3$.

Competing Limit Strategies - Version 1

Two approaches are proposed for $\lim_{x\to 3}\frac{x^2-9}{x-3}$. Approach A substitutes and reports DNE because $\frac00$ appears. Approach B factors and simplifies for nearby inputs. Compare the approaches, identify which is valid, and explain why it is more efficient.

Answer / Solution:Approach B is valid. $\frac00$ is indeterminate rather than DNE. Factoring gives $x+3$ for nearby $x\ne 3$, so the limit is $6$.
U1-FA-D3-01-V2

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

Section 1.4 | Formative Assessment Source Content | current-section | DOK 2

Piecewise Continuity Application - Version 1

Determine $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+k,&x\lt2\\2x+1,&x\ge2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=2$. Show the continuity equation.

Answer / Solution:$2+k=5$, so $k=3$.
U1-FA-D2-03-V2

Section 1.4 | Formative Assessment Source Content | current-section | DOK 3

Continuity Claim Critique - Version 1

A student says a function is continuous at $x=-2$ because the left- and right-hand limits both equal $3$. The graph also shows $f(-2)=6$. Evaluate the claim using all continuity conditions and classify the discontinuity.

Answer / Solution:The claim is false. The two-sided limit is $3$ but the function value is $6$. The discontinuity is removable.
U1-FA-D3-02-V2

Build a Continuity Model - Version 1

Build a piecewise model with these constraints:

  • continuous at $x=0$;
  • removable discontinuity at $x=2$ with limit $3$;
  • jump discontinuity at $x=4$.
  1. Write a complete formula with domains.
  2. Test all three boundaries using one-sided limits and point values.
  3. Provide a table or sketch as a second representation.
  4. Revise any branch that fails a constraint.
Answer / Solution:

Scoring: 4 points valid construction; 2 points analytic checks; 2 points second representation; 2 points documented verification/revision. Multiple functions can earn full credit.

  • jump discontinuity at $x=4$.
  1. Write a complete formula with domains.
  2. Test all three boundaries using one-sided limits and point values.
  3. Provide a table or sketch as a second representation.
  4. Revise any branch that fails a constraint.
Answer / Solution:

Scoring: 4 points valid construction; 2 points analytic checks; 2 points second representation; 2 points documented verification/revision. Multiple functions can earn full credit.

Continuity and Asymptote Vocabulary Check - Version 1

The left-hand limit is 2 and the right-hand limit is 5. Which description applies?

  1. removable discontinuity
  2. jump discontinuity
  3. vertical asymptote
  4. continuous at the point
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. Unequal finite one-sided limits indicate a jump.
U1-FA-D1-04-V2
Section 1.5 | Formative Assessment Source Content | current-section | DOK 1

Continuity and Asymptote Vocabulary Check - Version 2

The magnitude of $f(x)$ grows without bound as $x\to a$. Which feature is indicated?

  1. removable discontinuity
  2. jump discontinuity
  3. vertical asymptote
  4. continuous at the point
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: C. Unbounded nearby behavior indicates a vertical asymptote/infinite discontinuity.

  4. continuous at the point

Asymptote and IVT Application - Version 1

Consider $g(x)=-1+\frac{2}{x-2}$ and a continuous function $F$ on $[0,4]$ with $F(0)=-1$ and $F(4)=5$.

  1. Identify the vertical and horizontal asymptotes of $g$.
  2. Determine whether IVT guarantees an input $r$ with $F(r)=2$ and state why.
Answer / Solution:
  1. $x=2$ and $y=-1$.
  2. Yes. Continuity is given and 2 lies between -1 and 5.

IVT Hypothesis Justification - Version 1

A report asserts that IVT guarantees $F(r)=0$ for some $r\in(1,5)$. The known facts are $F(1)=-3$ and $F(5)=4$. The report also says $F$ is continuous except possibly at $x=3$. Decide whether the conclusion is guaranteed. Identify the theorem condition that controls your decision and state what additional evidence would settle it.

Answer / Solution:The conclusion is not yet guaranteed because continuity on the entire closed interval $[1,5]$ has not been verified. Evidence that $F$ is continuous at $x=3$ would complete the continuity hypothesis; the target already lies between the endpoint outputs.

Representation Synthesis for Limits - Version 1

Three pieces of information are reported near $x=-2$:

  • a graph sketch appears to approach $4$ from both sides;
  • a table from the left approaches $4$ while the first right-side row is $6$ but later right-side rows move toward $4$;
  • $f(-2)=9$.

Reconcile the evidence. State the most defensible limit conclusion and identify which observation should be checked before finalizing it.

Answer / Solution:The strongest overall evidence supports a limit of $4$. The early right-side table row should be checked because later right-side values and the graph trend toward $4$. The point value $9$ does not determine the limit.

Asymptotic Behavior Design Task - Version 1

Design a function representation with vertical asymptote $x=-2$ and horizontal asymptote $y=-1$. Require the graph to cross the horizontal asymptote at least once and to have no other vertical asymptote.

  1. Give a formula and domain.
  2. Verify all asymptotic claims with limits.
  3. Identify a crossing point or show numerically that one exists.
  4. Revise one parameter so the vertical asymptote moves one unit right while the horizontal asymptote stays fixed.
Answer / Solution:

Scoring: 3 points valid model; 3 points correct limit verification; 2 points crossing evidence; 2 points successful revision. Many models are possible.

Summative Assessment Source Content

Section 1.1: What Is a Limit?

U1-SUM-V1-Q01
Section 1.1 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 1 (No calculator)

Which statement best interprets $\lim_{x\to -2}f(x)=7$?

  1. $f(-2)=7$ must be true.
  2. As inputs approach $-2$ nearby outputs approach $7$.
  3. The input approaches $7$.
  4. The function is undefined near $-2$.
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. Limit notation describes nearby input/output approach and does not force the point value.
U1-SUM-V1-Q02
Section 1.1 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 2 (Calculator permitted as a check)

Use the table to estimate $\lim_{x\to -2}f(x)$.

xf(x)
-2.202.910
-2.052.978
-2.012.996
-1.993.005
-1.953.022
-1.803.090
  1. $3$
  2. $4$
  3. $-2$
  4. DNE
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. Values from both sides approach $3$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q03
Section 1.1 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 3 (No calculator)

A quantity changes from 3 units at $t=1$ to 10 units at $t=4$. Find the average rate of change and explain how shrinking the interval could be used to estimate an instantaneous rate at $t=1$.

Answer / Solution:Average rate $2.33333$ units per time unit. Instantaneous rate is estimated from secant slopes over progressively smaller intervals anchored near the time of interest.
U1-SUM-V1-Q04
Section 1.1 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 4 (No calculator)

Use the graph.

Which pair is correct for the two-sided limit and point value at $x=-2$?

  1. $\lim f=4$ and $f(-2)=7$
  2. $\lim f=7$ and $f(-2)=4$
  3. Both are $4$.
  4. The limit is DNE.
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. The curve approaches $4$ while the filled point is at $7$.

Section 1.2: Limits from Graphs and Tables

U1-SUM-V1-Q05
Section 1.2 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 5 (Calculator permitted as a check)

Use the table to determine the two-sided limit.

xf(x)
-2.201.950
-2.051.988
-2.011.998
-1.993.998
-1.953.990
-1.803.960
  1. $2$
  2. $4$
  3. DNE
  4. $3.0$
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: C. A two-sided limit exists only when the directional limits agree.
U1-SUM-V1-Q06
Section 1.2 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 6 (No calculator)

At $x=-2$ use the graph to determine the right-hand limit.

  1. $3$
  2. $5$
  3. $-2$
  4. DNE
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. Approaching from inputs greater than $-2$ follows the right branch toward $5$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q07
Section 1.2 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 7 (Calculator permitted)

A graph and calculator table are intended to represent the same local behavior. The graph approaches $5$ from both sides. The table approaches $5$ from the left but the right-side entries move toward $7$. Explain what conclusion can and cannot be justified until the discrepancy is resolved.

Answer / Solution:A final two-sided limit cannot be justified from conflicting representations. The right-side table setup or graph reading should be checked. The left-hand limit is supported as $5$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q08
Section 1.2 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 8 (No calculator)

Near $x=-2$ both sides approach $3$ but $f(-2)=8$. Which conclusion is correct?

  1. The two-sided limit is $8$.
  2. The two-sided limit is $3$.
  3. The two-sided limit is DNE because the values differ.
  4. The left-hand limit equals $8$.
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. The point value does not change the nearby two-sided approach.

Unit 1 Summative - Version 4 - Question 7 (Calculator permitted)

A graph and calculator table are intended to represent the same local behavior. The graph approaches $8$ from both sides. The table approaches $8$ from the left but the right-side entries move toward $10$. Explain what conclusion can and cannot be justified until the discrepancy is resolved.

Answer / Solution:A final two-sided limit cannot be justified from conflicting representations. The right-side table setup or graph reading should be checked. The left-hand limit is supported as $8$.
U1-SUM-V4-Q08
Section 1.2 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 4 - Question 8 (No calculator)

Near $x=2$ both sides approach $6$ but $f(2)=11$. Which conclusion is correct?

  1. The two-sided limit is $11$.
  2. The two-sided limit is $6$.
  3. The two-sided limit is DNE because the values differ.
  4. The left-hand limit equals $11$.
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. The point value does not change the nearby two-sided approach.
U1-SUM-V5-Q05
Section 1.2 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 5 - Question 5 (Calculator permitted as a check)

Use the table to determine the two-sided limit.

xf(x)
2.805.950
2.955.987
2.995.998
3.017.998
3.057.990
3.207.960
  1. $6$
  2. $8$
  3. DNE
  4. $7.0$
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: C. A two-sided limit exists only when the directional limits agree.
U1-SUM-V5-Q06
Section 1.2 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 5 - Question 6 (No calculator)

At $x=3$ use the graph to determine the right-hand limit.

  1. $7$
  2. $9$
  3. $3$
  4. DNE
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. Approaching from inputs greater than $3$ follows the right branch toward $9$.

Section 1.3: Algebraic Limits

U1-SUM-V1-Q09
Section 1.3 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 9 (No calculator)

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to2}(3x^2+3)$.

  1. $15$
  2. $12$
  3. $9$
  4. $\frac00$
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. Direct substitution gives $15$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q10
Section 1.3 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 10 (No calculator)

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to 3}\frac{x^2-9}{x-3}$.

  1. $6$
  2. $3$
  3. $0$
  4. DNE
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. Factor and cancel the common factor for nearby inputs.
U1-SUM-V1-Q11
Section 1.3 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 11 (No calculator)

Evaluate $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin(3x)}{x}$ and state the standard limit structure used.

Answer / Solution:The value is $3$, using $\lim_{u\to0}\frac{\sin u}{u}=1$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q12
Section 1.3 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 3

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 12 (No calculator)

A student substitutes into $\lim_{x\to 3}\frac{x^2-9}{x-3}$ and concludes DNE from $\frac00$. Critique the reasoning, complete a valid method, and explain why the method is legitimate for a limit.

Answer / Solution:$\frac00$ is indeterminate, not DNE. Factor and cancel $x-3$ for nearby $x\ne 3$ to obtain $x+3$; the limit is $6$.

Section 1.4: Continuity and Discontinuity

U1-SUM-V1-Q13
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 13 (No calculator)

At $x=-2$ both one-sided limits equal $4$ and $f(-2)=4$. Is $f$ continuous at the point?

  1. Yes
  2. No
  3. Cannot tell because one-sided limits are irrelevant
  4. Only if the graph has a horizontal asymptote
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. Continuity requires the common limit to equal the point value; here they agree.
U1-SUM-V1-Q14
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 14 (No calculator)

Find $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+k,&x\lt2\\3x+1,&x\ge2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=2$. Show the boundary equation.

Answer / Solution:$2+k=7$, so $k=5$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q15
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 15 (No calculator)

A discontinuity has this evidence: the left side approaches 2 and the right side approaches 6. Classify it.

  1. removable
  2. jump
  3. infinite
  4. continuous
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. The finite one-sided limits differ.
U1-SUM-V1-Q16
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 3

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 16 (No calculator)

A student argues that equality of one-sided limits is sufficient for continuity. At a test point the one-sided limits both equal $3$ while the function value is $7$. Critique the claim and state the minimal correction needed to make the function continuous at that point.

Answer / Solution:The claim omits the requirement $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=f(a)$. Redefine the point value to $3$ to remove the discontinuity.
U1-SUM-V2-Q13
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

  1. No
  2. Yes
  3. Cannot tell because one-sided limits are irrelevant
  4. Only if the graph has a horizontal asymptote

Find $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+k,&x\lt2\\4x+0,&x\ge2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=2$. Show the boundary equation.

Answer / Solution:$2+k=8$, so $k=6$.
U1-SUM-V2-Q15
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 2 - Question 15 (No calculator)

A discontinuity has this evidence: the magnitude of outputs grows without bound near the point. Classify it.

  1. removable
  2. jump
  3. infinite
  4. continuous
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: C. Nearby values are unbounded.

Unit 1 Summative - Version 3 - Question 14 (No calculator)

Find $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+k,&x\lt2\\2x+1,&x\ge2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=2$. Show the boundary equation.

Answer / Solution:$2+k=5$, so $k=3$.
U1-SUM-V3-Q15
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 3 - Question 15 (No calculator)

A discontinuity has this evidence: both sides approach 5 but the point value is 1. Classify it.

  1. removable
  2. jump
  3. infinite
  4. continuous
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. The two-sided limit exists but differs from the point value.
U1-SUM-V3-Q16
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 3

Unit 1 Summative - Version 3 - Question 16 (No calculator)

A student argues that equality of one-sided limits is sufficient for continuity. At a test point the one-sided limits both equal $5$ while the function value is $9$. Critique the claim and state the minimal correction needed to make the function continuous at that point.

Answer / Solution:The claim omits the requirement $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=f(a)$. Redefine the point value to $5$ to remove the discontinuity.
U1-SUM-V4-Q13
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 4 - Question 13 (No calculator)

At $x=2$ both one-sided limits equal $7$ and $f(2)=9$. Is $f$ continuous at the point?

  1. No
  2. Yes
  3. Cannot tell because one-sided limits are irrelevant
  4. Only if the graph has a horizontal asymptote
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. Continuity requires the common limit to equal the point value; here they differ.
U1-SUM-V4-Q14
Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 4 - Question 14 (No calculator)

Find $k$ so $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+k,&x\lt2\\3x+0,&x\ge2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $x=2$. Show the boundary equation.

Answer / Solution:$2+k=6$, so $k=4$.
U1-SUM-V4-Q15

Unit 1 Summative - Version 4 - Question 15 (No calculator)

A discontinuity has this evidence: the left side approaches 2 and the right side approaches 6. Classify it.

  1. removable
  2. jump
  3. infinite
  4. continuous
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: B. The finite one-sided limits differ.

Section 1.4 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 5 - Question 15 (No calculator)

A discontinuity has this evidence: the magnitude of outputs grows without bound near the point. Classify it.

  1. removable
  2. jump
  3. infinite
  4. continuous
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: C. Nearby values are unbounded.

Section 1.5: Asymptotes and IVT

U1-SUM-V1-Q17
Section 1.5 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 17 (No calculator)

For $g(x)=\frac{3x^2+1}{x^2+3}$ identify the horizontal asymptote.

  1. $y=3$
  2. $y=0$
  3. $x=3$
  4. $y=0.333$
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. Equal degrees give the ratio of leading coefficients $3$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q18
Section 1.5 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 2

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 18 (No calculator)

$F$ is continuous on $[0,5]$ with $F(0)=-2$ and $F(5)=6$. Does IVT guarantee an input where $F(x)=2$?

  1. Yes because the target lies between the endpoint outputs.
  2. No because IVT applies only to derivatives.
  3. No because a continuous function cannot cross the target.
  4. Yes even if continuity were removed.
Answer / Solution:Correct answer: A. Continuity is given and the target lies between -2 and 6.
U1-SUM-V1-Q19
Section 1.5 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 3

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 19 (No calculator)

For $h(x)=-1+\frac{3}{x-2}$ write the two one-sided infinite limits at $x=2$ and both end-behavior limits. Then identify the vertical and horizontal asymptotes.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to 2^-}h(x)=-\infty$, $\lim_{x\to 2^+}h(x)=\infty$, and both end limits equal $-1$. Asymptotes: $x=2$ and $y=-1$.
U1-SUM-V1-Q20
Section 1.5 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 3

Unit 1 Summative - Version 1 - Question 20 (No calculator)

A function has endpoint values $G(1)=-4$ and $G(6)=5$, but a vertical asymptote occurs at $x=3$. A student invokes IVT to guarantee $G(r)=0$ for some $r\in(1,6)$. Evaluate the theorem use and state a valid revision of the claim.

Answer / Solution:The theorem use is invalid on $[1,6]$ because the vertical asymptote breaks continuity. A valid revision must restrict IVT to a subinterval on one side of 3 where continuity is verified and whose endpoint outputs straddle the target.
U1-SUM-V2-Q17
Section 1.5 | Summative Assessment Source Content | unit-synthesis | DOK 1

Unit 1 Summative - Version 8 - Question 19 (No calculator)

For $h(x)=1+\frac{3}{x-1}$ write the two one-sided infinite limits at $x=1$ and both end-behavior limits. Then identify the vertical and horizontal asymptotes.

Answer / Solution:$\lim_{x\to 1^-}h(x)=-\infty$, $\lim_{x\to 1^+}h(x)=\infty$, and both end limits equal $1$. Asymptotes: $x=1$ and $y=1$.

Unit 1 Summative - Version 8 - Question 20 (No calculator)

A function has endpoint values $G(1)=-4$ and $G(6)=5$, but a vertical asymptote occurs at $x=3$. A student invokes IVT to guarantee $G(r)=2$ for some $r\in(1,6)$. Evaluate the theorem use and state a valid revision of the claim.

Answer / Solution:The theorem use is invalid on $[1,6]$ because the vertical asymptote breaks continuity. A valid revision must restrict IVT to a subinterval on one side of 3 where continuity is verified and whose endpoint outputs straddle the target.
